Bi-accessible and bipresentable 2-categories
Ivan Di Liberti Axel Osmond

Abstract
We develop a 2-dimensional version of accessibility and presentability compatible with the formalism of flat pseudofunctors. First we give prerequisites on the different notions of 2-dimensional colimits, filteredness and cofinality; in particular we show that σ \sigma -filteredness and bifilteredness are actually equivalent in practice for our purposes. Then, we define bi-accessible and bipresentable 2-categories in terms of bicompact objects and bifiltered bicolimits. We then characterize them as categories of flat pseudofunctors . We also prove a bi-accessible right bi-adjoint functor theorem and deduce a 2-dimensional Gabriel-Ulmer duality relating small bilex 2-categories and finitely bipresentable 2-categories. Finally, we show that 2-categories of pseudo-algebras of finitary 2-monads on 𝐂𝐚𝐭 {\bf Cat} are finitely bipresentable, which in particular captures the case of 𝐋𝐞𝐱 {\bf Lex} , the 2-category of small lex categories. Invoking the technology of lex-colimits , we prove further that several 2 2 -categories arising in categorical logic ( Reg, Ex, Coh, Ext, Adh, Pretop ) are also finitely bipresentable. Keywords. bi-accessible 2 2 -category, bipresentable 2 2 -category, bifiltered bicolimit, bicompact object, doctrine. MSC2020. 18C35; 18N10; 18A30; 18C10; 18D65.



Introduction

The original motivation of this work was to find a 2-dimensional notion of presentability encompassing the different finitary
first order doctrines
corresponding to different fragments of first order logic. Those are 2-categories whose objects are small categories endowed with a certain structure allowing to see them as syntactic categories for first order theories, where one can interpret connectors and inference rules. Prominent examples of doctrines are
Lex
, for
left exact
categories, corresponding to cartesian logic;
Reg
, for
regular
categories, corresponding to regular logic;
Coh
for
coherent categories
, corresponding to coherent logic; but also the 2-categories
Ext
of
extensive
categories,
Adh
of
adhesive
categories,
Ex
of
exact
categories,
\textbf{Pretop}_{\omega}
of
finitary pretopoi
, or
BoolPretop
of
boolean
finitary pretopoi.

Those doctrines can also be seen as higher-dimensional versions of the different categories of propositional algebras, as
\wedge\mbox{-}\textbf{Slat}
, the category of meet-semilattices,
DLat
, the category of bounded distributive lattices,
Bool
the category of boolean algebras and so on. A common feature to most of those categories of propositional algebras is that they are
finitely presentable
: they are cocomplete and generated under filtered colimits by an essentially small subcategory of compact objects. Finitely presentable categories are known to enjoy a lot of excellent properties and provide a framework generalizing universal algebra, a reason for which the 1-categorical theory of presentability, as well as the more general theory of
accessibility
, have become classical topics at the intersection of category theory and model theory since
[
MP89
]
and
[
AR94
]
. Already in
[
Mak95
]
, Makkai envisions the relevance of this project, prospecting that a general theory of
2
-dimensional accessibility and presentability should exist and should extend to the
2
-categories of interest in this paper
1
1

1

In
[
Mak95
]
, Makkai claims that much of this work should be straightforward, yet we will discuss a few paragraphs below that
the devil is in the details
.
.

Previous proposal for 2-dimensional accessibility and presentability can be found in
[
Kel82
]
and
[
Bou20
]
in the stricter context of enriched categories. However, capturing first order doctrines as examples requires a more relaxed version involving weaker notions of filteredness and colimits: for instance,
Lex
is not 2-presentable in the sense of
[
Kel82
]
because it has only bicolimits and not all strict ones, beside issues about its expected rank of 2-accessibility in the sense of
[
Bou20
]
. Here, we will work in the environment of strict 2-categories together with pseudofunctors and pseudonatural transformation (or equivalently, strict 2-functors and pseudonatural transformations), as our notions will involve weak inversal properties relying on equivalences of categories rather than isomorphisms; a good reference for this framework is provided by
[
JY21
]
.

We introduce here relaxed notions of
bi-accessible
(
Definition

3.2.1
) and
bipresentable
(
Definition

3.3.1
) 2-categories and connect them to the recent advance of
[
DDS18
]
on the theory of
flat pseudofunctors
. Our notion relies on
[
Ken92
]
notion of
bifilteredness
, together with a convenient notion of
bicompact objects
(
Definition

3.1.1
) enjoying the analogous property of compact objects against bifiltered bicolimits; we define finitely bi-accessible categories as those having bifiltered bicolimits and an essentially small subcategory of bicompact objects generating them under bifiltered bicolimits; finitely bipresentable 2-categories are as those that are moreover bicocomplete - but similarly to the one dimensional case, this amounts to having weighted bilimits. In particular, a handy criterion to detect finite bipresentability of a
2
-category is offered in
Theorem

3.4.3
, which is a
2
-dimensional analog of a classical characterization through strong generators. We then prove that categories of flat pseudofunctors are bi-accessible (
Corollary

4.2.6
) and bipresentable (
Theorem

4.3.6
) if their domain admits finite weighted bilimits, the latter result being part of a categorification of the well known
Gabriel-Ulmer duality
established in
Section

5.3
.

A subtle part of this work is to chose first the appropriate shape of 2-dimensional filteredness and which associated classes of 2-dimensional (co)-limits should be used to express accessibility. There are two competing possible choices:

-

one either could rely on (conical) bifiltered bicolimits as mentioned above à la
[
Ken92
]
, which looks as the most naive possible generalization; the problem with this approach is that
{\bf Cat}
-valuated pseudofunctors do not express in general as conical bicolimits of representables, which a priori is an obstruction to a theory of flat pseudofunctors;

-

or one could either use the formalism of
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-colimits
, a class intermediate between bi and lax. The point with this formulation is that it goes with an already established, nicely behaved theory of flat pseudofunctors, as developed in
[
DDS18
]
. This theoretical advantage qualifies this formalism as a
conceptually
correct notion of 2-dimensional filteredness.

Luckily, those two approaches actually merge into a single one thanks to the key observation that any
\sigma
-filtered 2-category admits a
\sigma
-cofinal
bifiltered 2-category (see
Lemma

2.2.6
) which simplifies the corresponding
\sigma
-bicolimit into an ordinary bicolimit. Those simplifications can also be observed by the fact that all non invertible oplax inclusions of a
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-colimit can be “swallowed” by the diagram and replaced by invertible inclusion 2-cells (
Lemma

2.1.10
).

In particular this explains why, though defined with bifiltered bicolimits, bi-accessible 2-category corresponds to 2-categories of flat pseudofunctors which where first defined in
[
DDS18
]
in terms of
\sigma
-filteredness and
\sigma
-colimits. This phenomenon happens to be related also to the 2-dimensional part of the lifting property of bicompact objects, which allows to transfer non-invertible 2-cells of a
\sigma
-cocone into the underlying diagram. It also ensures that the 2-dimensional analog of the canonical cocone over an object in a bi-accessible 2-category can be chosen either as the
\sigma
-filtered oplax cocone (relative to the cocartesian 1-cells, see
3.2.3
) or more simply as a bifiltered bicocone over it (see
Lemma

3.2.5
). All of this ensures that any attempt to do a theory of “
\sigma
-accessible 2-categories” uniquely stated in term of
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits will automatically reduce to our theory of bi-accessibility.

Our last section is devoted to the motivating examples. After the preliminary observation that
{\bf Cat}
really is the ur-example of finitely bipresentable 2-categories, we prove in
Theorem

6.2.3
that the 2-category of pseudo-algebras and pseudomorphisms for a bifinitary pseudomonad on a finitely bipresentable 2-category is itself finitely bipresentable. This captures in particular the example of
{\bf Lex}
(see
Lemma

6.3.2
), for its bifiltered bicolimits can be shown to be computed in
{\bf Cat}
. Incidentally, this argument applies also to the two category of monoidal categories and strong monoidal functors (see
6.3.6
).

Proving the remaining finitary first order doctrines to be finitely bipresentable could be achieved in several ways. One could directly try to manipulate their axioms as kind of “2-dimensional cartesian predicates” (involving only finitely weighted bilimits or left and right Kan extensions) in a manner reminiscent of Makkai paradigm of
injectivity classes
. While we believe such a work to be interesting by itself as an occasion to conscientiously perform 2-dimensional logic, such a process would have represented an enormous amount of specific considerations and lemmas for each example. Such a tedious work appeared superfluous thanks to the powerful paradigm of
lex colimits
introduced by
[
GL12
]
, which was specifically developed to capture a large class of examples of doctrines defined through different flavours of exactness conditions. Here they are axiomatized as classes of pseudo-algebras for some 2-monad on
{\bf Lex}
defined as a free cocompletion under specific colimits weighted in some class of finite weights - this has been described as “cocompleteness in the lex world”. Having proven
{\bf Lex}
to be finitely bipresentable, it suffices then to prove the embedding of the category of those pseudo-algebras (which form the corresponding 2-categories of exact categories relative to those weights) to be bifinitary, which is done in
Lemma

6.4.4
. This captures all the remaining doctrines defined from exactness properties as
Reg
,
Ex
,
Coh
,
Adh
,
Ext
and
\textbf{Pretop}_{\omega}
.

1
Prerequisites

1.1
Notions of strictness and universality

In this subsection we shall recall some general definitions and facts about
2
-dimensional category theory, mainly addressing the strictness nuances that will arise later in the paper. We briefly recall the notion of weighted (bi)limit. We refer to
[
JY21
]
for an introduction to the topic, but we address the reader to
[
Bou20
, Sec. 3.4]
and
[
BKPS89
, Sec. 1 and 2]
, for a very detailed discussion and comparison with the literature.

1.1.1
2
-Categories of
2
-functors.

For any 2-categories
\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{B}
we have the following inclusions of 2-categories of strict 2-functors together with respectively
strict
,
pseudo
and
lax
natural transformations:

[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]_{p}\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]_{l}.

However, we are going to work mostly with
pseudofunctors
between 2-categories: whence the importance of the following observation, from
[
JY21
]
, ensuring that considering pseudofunctors between strict 2-categories returns actually again a strict 2-category rather than the weaker notion of bicategory - which we are not going to consider in this work:

Proposition 1.1.2

(
[
JY21
]
[Corollary 4.4.13])
.

Let be
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
two 2-categories; then taking pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations between them and natural modifications between those yields a 2-category
{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]
.

Proposition 1.1.3

(Strictification of pseudofunctors, originally shown in
[
Pow89
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a strict 2-category, then any pseudofunctor
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
admits a
strictification
, that is there exists a strict 2-functor
\overline{F}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
and a pseudonatural equivalence
\eta_{F}:F\Rightarrow\overline{F}
. Moreover for any strict 2-functor
G:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
we have an equivalence of categories,

{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A},{\bf Cat}][F,\iota(G)]\simeq[\mathcal{A},{\bf Cat}][\overline{F},G].

1.1.4
Notions of
2
-limits.

Let
I
be a 2-category. For a weight, that is a pseudofunctor
W:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
, and a pseudofunctor
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
we denote respectively as
{\textup{pslim}}^{\!W}F
and
{\textup{bilim}}^{W}F
the
W
-weighted
pseudolimit and bilimit, whose universal properties are respectively that for any
B
in
\mathcal{B}
we have a natural isomorphism, resp. a natural equivalence of categories

\mathcal{B}[B,{\textup{pslim}}^{W}F]\cong{\bf ps}[I,{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[B,F]\big]

\mathcal{B}[B,{\textup{bilim}}^{W}F]\simeq{\bf ps}[I,{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[B,F]\big]

Similarly for a contravariant weight
W:I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
a pseudofunctor we define respectively the
W
-weighted
pseudocolimit

{\textup{pscolim}}^{W}F
and
bicolimit

{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}F
from the universal property that for any
B
we have an isomorphism, resp. an equivalence of categories

\mathcal{B}[{\textup{pscolim}}^{W}F,B]\cong{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[F,B]\big]

\mathcal{B}[{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}F,B]\simeq{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[F,B]\big]

In particular, one recovers the corresponding
conical
kind of (co)limits by considering the terminal weight
\Delta_{1}:I\rightarrow Cat
sending any object of
I
to the one object category 1.

Definition 1.1.5

(Finite weighted bilimits)
.

A
finite weight
(in the sense of
[
DDS18
, Def. 3.2.2]
) is a weight
W:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
such that
\mathcal{A}
is an essentially finite category and each
W(A)
is an essentially finite category. A
finite limit
is a weighted bilimit with finite weight.

Example 1.1.6
.

It immediately follows from the definition that finite biproducts, biequalizers and bicotensors with the arrow category 2 are all finite weighted bilimits. It is observed in
[
DDS18
, Rem. 3.2.5]
that these suffice to construct all finite weighted bilimits.

Remark 1.1.7

(On finite
2
-limits)
.

Depending on the flavor of mathematics, the intentions of the paper and the historical moment, people have used different notions of
finiteness
for
2
-limits. Let us review those that are most related to ours.

-

In
[
Str76
, p. 149 and (more importantly) 150]
, Street provides one of the first historical discussions on the notion of finite
2
-limit. There a
2
-category is said to be
finitely complete
when it has
products, equalizers, and cotensors with the arrow category
. The notion of finite limit is based on the notion of
finite presentable category
. Of course, Street shows that a category is finitely complete if and only if it has all finite
2
-limits. This notion of
2
-limit will be later used

[
Str82b
]
to characterize Street’s notion of
2
-dimensional topos. Street’s theory is very
strict
, and thus can be only considered inspirational for us.
This theory may lead to a strict version of Diaconescu theorem, while
[
DDS18
]
result on extension of flat functors is stated in terms of finite bilimits. All in all, the notions of finite limit in
[
Str82b
]
and
[
Str76
]
are strict and based on
finite presentable categories
, this makes it incomparable to our framework.

-

[
Str82a
]
clarifies how to technically manipulate the theory of
[
Str82b
]
and
[
Str76
]
to characterize bitopoi and thus meets our framework in a comparable way. His notion of finite (bi)limits is still based on
[
Str76
]
, with the exception of the strictness of the universal property. Thus, finite bilimits in the sense of
[
Str82a
]
are more than finite bilimits in our sense. Yet, because finite bilimits in the sense of Street are generated by
biproducts, biequalizers, and bicotensors with the arrow category

[
Str76
, analog of Cor. 8]
, his notion of lex functor is equivalent to ours via
Example

1.1.6
.

-

The last notion we would like to mention is in
[
Kel82
, Def. 4.1]
.
[
Kel82
, 4.6]
makes this theory collapse to
[
Str76
]
in the case of
{\bf Cat}
-enriched categories, at least for what concerns the notion of lexity.

1.2
\sigma
-limits

Now we turn to an intermediate class of limits between the pseudo and lax, identified first in
[
DDS18
]
: it is the universal (op)lax cone where one requires only some 2-cells inclusions to be invertible as in a bilimit. In particular, this definition is always relative to a choice of a class of maps in the indexing 2-category. In this section, we carefully recall the main ideas of the theory of
\sigma
-limits from
[
DDS18
]
and
[
DDS16
]
. More recently, this notion has been revisited from another perspective in
[
GHL21
]
under the name of
marked limits
.

Remark 1.2.1
.

Our definition of
\sigma
-limit is slightly more general at first sight from that of
[
DDS16
]
, as they only define the notion of
\sigma
-limit for a strict
2
-functor, while we will need it for pseudofunctors too for we will have to consider
\sigma
-limits of composite of strict 2-functors along pseudofunctors. Such a level of generality does not change the theory in expressivity, and we shall provide a lemma to prove it (see
Lemma

1.2.14
).

Definition 1.2.2

(
\sigma
-natural transformations)
.

Let
I
be a 2-category and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
containing equivalences and closed under composition and invertible 2-cells; let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
F,G:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a pair of pseudofunctors. A
\sigma
-
natural transformation
relatively to
\Sigma
is a lax natural transformation
f:F\Rightarrow G
whose lax naturality squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}

at an arrow
s
in
\Sigma
are invertible 2-cells of
\mathcal{C}
. Similarly an
op
\sigma
-
natural transformation
is an oplax natural transformation whose oplax naturality squares over maps in
\Sigma
are invertible. We denote as
{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\Sigma}
the 2-category of pseudofunctors and
\sigma
-natural transformations relatively to
\Sigma
, with no restriction on 2-cells, and
{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}
for op
\sigma
-natural transformations.

Definition 1.2.3
.

For any
I
and any class of map
\Sigma
in
I
(without assumption about
\Sigma
), one can consider the closure
\overline{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
defined as the smallest sub-2-category containing
\Sigma
and closed under invertible 2-cells.

1.2.4
.

It is clear that any
\overline{\Sigma}
-natural transformation is in particular
\Sigma
-natural, so we have for each pairs of pseudofunctors
F,G:I\rightarrow\Sigma
a full inclusion
{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\overline{\Sigma}}\hookrightarrow{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\Sigma}[F,G]
, and similarly for op-
\sigma
-natural transformations. But in fact, the coherence conditions of (op)lax transformations ensure that any (op-)
\Sigma
-natural transformation is automatically (op-)
\overline{\Sigma}
-natural, and more formally:

Lemma 1.2.5
.

Let be
\Sigma
a class of maps in a 2-category
I
. Then we have a biequivalence of 2-categories, pseudonatural in
C
:

{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\Sigma}\simeq{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\overline{\Sigma}}\hskip 14.22636pt(\textrm{resp. }{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}\simeq{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{{\rm op}\overline{\Sigma}})

Proof.

For there are no restrictions on pseudofunctors, those 2-categories have the same objects. It suffices then to prove for any two
F,G
that the full inclusion
{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\overline{\Sigma}}\hookrightarrow{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{C}]_{\Sigma}[F,G]
is actually surjective on objects: we show as promised that any
\Sigma
-natural transformation
f:F\Rightarrow G
is actually
\overline{\Sigma}
-natural. First, take
s:i\rightarrow j
and
t:j\rightarrow k
in
\Sigma
and a pair: then lax naturality gives us the equality of 2-cells

Hence the composite
f_{k}*F^{-1}_{s,t}f_{ts}
is invertible, and so is
f_{ts}
by cancellation of invertible 2-cell. Similar argument for identity arrows. Finally suppose that
\alpha:d\simeq s
is an invertible 2-cell with
s
in
\Sigma
, than in the same vein one has an equality of 2-cells

Then again cancellation of invertible 2-cells ensures that
f_{d}
has to be invertible.
∎

Remark 1.2.6
.

As a consequence, one can always stipulate
\Sigma
to be closed under the condition above; in this case
\Sigma
is in particular a full on 0-cells and 2-cells sub-2-category of
I
.

Definition 1.2.7

(
\sigma
-cone)
.

Let
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a pseudofunctor and
\Sigma
a class in
I
. Then a
\sigma
-
cone
on
F
(relative to
\Sigma
) is a lax cone on
C
in
\mathcal{C}
is a lax natural transformation
f:\Delta_{B}\Rightarrow F
whose lax naturality triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{d}}

which are in particular invertible whenever
d
is in
\Sigma
. Dually, a
\sigma
-cocone
is an
op
\sigma
-natural transformation
q:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{B}
, with oplax naturality triangles invertible at maps in
\Sigma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}}

Remark 1.2.8

(Cones are lax, cocones are oplax)
.

The reader might be confused by the orientation of the
2
-dimensional data in the diagrams above: the
\sigma
-cones are made of lax cells below an object, while
\sigma
-cocones are made of
oplax
cells above it. The opsigma naturality condition involved is explained at
[
DDS18
, Remark 2.4.1]
. We will also give some justifications at the definition of
\sigma
-colimit in
1.2.12
.

Remark 1.2.9
.

Observe that any lax cone (resp. any pseudocone) is a
\sigma
-cone with
\Sigma
consisting only of isomorphisms in
I
(resp.
\Sigma
containing all arrows of
I
). Dually, a pseudocone is a
\sigma
-cone for a
\Sigma
consisting of all arrows.

Definition 1.2.10

(Weighted
\sigma
-bilimit)
.

Let
I
be a small 2-category and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
containing equivalences and closed under composition and invertible 2-cells,
W:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a pseudofunctor and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
a pseudofunctor. Then the
weighted
\sigma
-limit
(resp.
\sigma
-bilimit
) relatively to
\Sigma

such that for any
B
in
\mathcal{B}
we have an isomorphism (resp. an equivalence) of categories

\mathcal{B}[B,\Sigma\lim\,F]\cong{\bf ps}[I,{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma}\big[W,\mathcal{B}[B,F]\big]

\mathcal{B}[B,\Sigma{\textup{bilim}}\,F]\simeq{\bf ps}[I,{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma}\big[W,\mathcal{B}[B,F]\big]

Remark 1.2.11

(Conical
\sigma
-(bi)limits)
.

In particular we can consider conical
\sigma
-(bi)limits as those
\sigma
-cones

(p_{i}:\Sigma\underset{i\in I}{\lim}\,F(i)\rightarrow F(i))_{i\in I}\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{( resp. }(p_{i}:\Sigma\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bilim}}}\,F(i)\rightarrow F(i))_{i\in I}\textup{)}

such that any other
\sigma
-cone
(f_{i}:B\rightarrow F(i))_{i\in I}
induces a universal arrow
(f_{i})_{i\in I}:B\rightarrow\Sigma\lim\,F
commuting strictly, reps. up to a canonical invertible 2-cell, with the cone projections.

1.2.12
Weighted
\sigma
-(bi)colimits.

We can also dually define weighted
\sigma
-(bi)colimit
for a weight
I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
with
(I,\Sigma)
a
\Sigma
pair and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
through the formula

\mathcal{B}[\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}}\,F(i),B]\simeq{\bf ps}[{I}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma^{{\rm op}}}\big[W,\mathcal{B}[F,B]\big]

In particular, in the case of conical bicolimits, that is for the weight
W=\Delta_{1}
, observe also that the later homcategory is equivalent to the category of
\sigma
-cocones over
F
with tip
B

[{I}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma^{{\rm op}}}\big[\Delta_{1},\mathcal{B}[F,B]\big]\simeq{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{B}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}[F,\Delta_{B}]

In particular, this exhibits the
\sigma
-bicolimit as a universal
\sigma
-cocone; beware that its 2-cells inclusions at a morphism
d:i\rightarrow j
in
I
are of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}}

Remark 1.2.13

(Avoiding weights)
.

As one interest of
\sigma
-(co)limits is that they allow to turn weighted (co)limits into special conical lax (co)limits, for instance in the
\sigma
-colimit decomposition into representable we shall see below, we will only make use of conical
\sigma
-(co)limits in the following. We will mostly use also the bi(co)limits, yet in some contexts - in particular in
{\bf Cat}
- we may innocently interchange pseudolimits with bilimits when the former have a canonical expression, using that, whenever they both exist, they are equivalent.

The next lemma ensures that there are no difference between our theory of sigma-bicolimits of pseudofunctors and
[
DDS18
]
version for strict 2-functors:

Lemma 1.2.14
.

Suppose that
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
is a pseudofunctor admitting a strictification
p_{F}:F\Rightarrow\overline{F}
. Take
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
. Then the strictification induces an equivalence:

\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F\simeq\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\overline{F}

Proof.

This is simply because the strictification
p_{F}
is a pseudonatural equivalence and hence induces a pseudonatural equivalence at the level of the hom-categories

{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{B}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}[F,\Delta_{B}]\simeq{\bf ps}[I,\mathcal{B}]_{{\rm op}\Sigma}[\overline{F},\Delta_{B}]

Hence if those pseudofunctors are representables, the representing objects are equivalent in an essentially unique way: hence the
\sigma
-bicolimits of
F
and of its strictification are equivalent.
∎

As the theory of
\sigma
-limits is very recent and lax limits are not as well documented as pseudo-limits or strict 2-limits, it is worth giving a few lemmas to ensure they can be manipulated as expected.

Lemma 1.2.15
.

Right bi-adjoints preserve weighted
\sigma
-bilimits. Respectively, left bi-adjoints preserve weighted
\sigma
-bicolimits.

Proof.

Let
L\dashv R
be a biadjunction with
R:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
, a 2-category
I
with
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
,
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
W:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a weight. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
have weighted
\sigma
-limits. Then one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[B,R(\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bilim}}^{W}}\,F(i))]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{A}[LB,\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bilim}}^{W}}\,F(i)]

\displaystyle\simeq[I,{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma}\big[W,\mathcal{A}[LB,F(i)]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq[I,{\bf Cat}]_{\Sigma}\big[W,\mathcal{B}[B,RF(i)]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[B,\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bilim}}^{W}}\,RF(i)]

∎

1.2.16
\sigma
-bicolimits of categories as fractions of oplax-colimits.

It is worth recalling the computation of conical
\sigma
-bicolimits of categories according to
[
DDS18
, Subsec. 2.5]
. For a
\sigma
-pair
(I,\Sigma)
and a pseudofunctor
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
, the
\sigma
-colimit is obtained as the localization of the oplax colimit at cocartesian lifts of
\Sigma
-arrows. If one defines

\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}=\textbf{coCart}_{F}\cap\pi_{F}^{-1}(\Sigma)

with
\pi_{F}:{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I}F(i)\rightarrow I
the associated fibration (recall that
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}_{i\in I}F(i)
is the classifying category of the Grothendieck construction of
F
), one has the equation below. Moreover this
\sigma
-bicolimit has actually the universal property of a
\sigma
-colimit.

\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{oplaxcolim}}}\,F(i)[\Sigma_{(F,\Sigma)}^{-1}].

Remark 1.2.17

(Equalization of parallel vertical pairs)
.

Here we should give an observation about when two morphisms in a member of the
\sigma
-bicolimit are identified in the
\sigma
-bicolimit itself after applying the inclusion to them: this will be of use later. The class
\Sigma_{F,\Sigma}
admits a
left calculus of fractions
where a free arrow is created for each cospan of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,F(s)(a^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{F(s)(a^{\prime})})}

with
s
in
\Sigma
. Moreover one can characterize when two such spans are equivalent in the localization, see the left dual of proposition
[
Bor94
]
[Proposition 5.2.4 (3)] which stands for a right calculus of fractions. In particular if we apply this formula to the case of a parallel pair of the specific form
(1_{i},\phi),(1_{i},\phi^{\prime}):(i,a)\rightrightarrows(i,a^{\prime})
(that is, coming from a parallel pair in
F(i)
), then saying they are equivalent in the localization amounts to saying there exists
s:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
such that the corresponding cocartesian morphism equalizes them

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,F(s)(a^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{i},\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{i},\phi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,1_{F(s)(a^{\prime})})}

But this latter condition exactly means that
F(s)(\phi)=F(s)(\phi)^{\prime}
in
F(i^{\prime})
. This observation will have useful consequences in the study of the specific case of
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits
of categories.

Example 1.2.18
.

In
{\bf Cat}
, pseudolimits can be taken for bilimits, and are computed as follows. For
I
a small 2-category and
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a pseudofunctor, the conical pseudolimit
{\textup{pslim}}\,F
has as objects pairs
((A_{i})_{i\in I},(\alpha_{d})_{d\in I^{2}})
with
A_{i}\in F(i)
and for each
d:i\rightarrow j
in
I
,
\alpha_{d}:F(i)\simeq F(j)
an isomorphism, such that moreover we have the cocycle identities

\alpha_{1_{i}}=1_{A_{i}}\quad\alpha_{d_{1}d_{2}}=\alpha_{d_{1}}\alpha_{d_{2}}

They are the case of a
\sigma
-bicolimit where all arrows are in
\Sigma
.

1.3
BiKan extensions

In this subsection we recall biKan extensions. We follow the treatment of
[
DDS18
, Sec. 4]
, originally inspired by
[
Nun16
]
.

Definition 1.3.1

(Left biKan extension)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
be and
G:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
two pseudofunctors. Then the
left biKan extension of
F
along
G
is the following 2-cell in
2
-
{\bf Cat}
with the universal property that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

-

for any other pseudonatural transformation
\zeta:F\Rightarrow HG
there exists an essentially unique pseudonatural transformation
\xi:{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F\Rightarrow H
such that we have a canonical invertible modification
\zeta\simeq\xi*G\lambda

for any
H:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
and any natural modification
\phi:\zeta\Rrightarrow\zeta^{\prime}
in the homcategory
{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}][F,HG]
, there is a unique modification
\psi
such that
\phi=(\psi*G)*\lambda
as depicted below

}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.27205pt}{-6.99669pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

In other words, we have for each
H:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
an equivalence between homcategories

{\bf ps}[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}][{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F,H]\simeq{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}][F,HG]

We can also define the
right biKan extension
, denoted
\textup{biRan}_{G}F
, which has the expected dual property.

Moreover, whenever they exist, left biKan extensions can be computed as weighted bicolimits: this is essentially the content of
[
DDS18
, 4.1.5,4.1.6]
:

Proposition 1.3.2
.

When
\mathcal{B}
has small bicolimits, we can compute the left biKan extension for any
C

in
\mathcal{C}
as the bicolimit

{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F(C)={\textup{bicolim}}^{\mathcal{N}_{G}(C)}F

where
\mathcal{N}_{G}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
sends
C
on the contravariant weight
\mathcal{C}[G,C]:\mathcal{A}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
.

1.3.3
Cancellation rule for left biKan extensions.

Now, let
\mathcal{A}
be a small
2
-category, and consider an extension problem as above. Another way to express the formula above is given by the following diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A}^{\rm op},{\bf Cat}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{biLan}_{G}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}[G,-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{biLan}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G*}

where the leftmost 2-cell
G*
is the pseudonatural transformation induced by
G
between homsets, while the rightmost 2-cell happens to be a pseudonatural equivalence. Indeed the
{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F
, when applied to a weight
W
gives us the bicolimit along the weight, and thus we have the following equation

{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F(W)\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}F,

which thus gives us the formula

{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F\simeq{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}F\circ\mathcal{C}[G,-]

This observation suggests that a vast class of functors admits indeed the computation of a left Kan extenstion. Indeed, if the functor
\mathcal{C}[G,-]
lands in the full subcategory of weights which are small bicolimits of representables, we can still use the formula above and compute the biKan extension, because we assume
\mathcal{B}
to have small weighted bicolimits.

Remark 1.3.4
.

We also have the dual formula for the computation of the right biKan extension

{\textup{biRan}}_{G}F\simeq{\textup{biRan}}_{\text{\char 232\relax}^{{\rm op}}}F\circ\mathcal{C}[-,G]

with
\text{\char 232\relax}:\mathcal{A}^{{\rm op}}\hookrightarrow{\bf ps}[\mathcal{A},{\bf Cat}]
being the contravariant 2-dimensional Yoneda embedding.

Proposition 1.3.5

(On the existence of biKan extensions)
.

In the notation of
1.3.3
, if
\mathcal{A}
is a (possibly large)
2
-category,
\mathcal{B}
has small weighted bicolimits and the functor
\mathcal{C}[G,-]
lands in the full subcategory of weights which are small bicolimits of representables, the
{\textup{biLan}}_{G}F
exists and is computed as in
1.3.3
.

Proof.

Follows from the discussion in
1.3.3
.
∎

Proposition 1.3.6
.

Suppose in the above context that
G
is pseudofully faithful; then the canonical pseudonatural transformation
\lambda:F\Rightarrow{\textup{biLan}}_{G}FG
is a point-wise equivalence.

Proof.

[
DDS18
, Prop. 4.1.10]

∎

1.4
Bi-adjunction and bireflectiveness

It is well known that reflective subcategories inherit limits. Here we want to prove the corresponding result for bilimits in the context of bireflective sub-bicategories.

Proposition 1.4.1
.

Let
R:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
be a pseudo-fully faithful pseudofunctor with a bi-adjoint
L\dashv R
. Then if
\mathcal{B}
has bilimit,
\mathcal{A}
is closed in
\mathcal{B}
under bilimits.

This proposition is general abstract nonsense for which we did not find a direct reference in the literature: we leave it as an exercise for the reader (hint: categorify
[
Bor94
]
[Proposition 3.5.3].)

Remark 1.4.2
.

By duality, we also have that bicoreflective sub-bicategories, that are, those
L:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
that have a right bi-adjoint, inherit bicolimits. We are going to use this form later.

We also know that reflective sub-2-categories inherit bicolimits - though they might not be preserved by the forgetful functor:

Proposition 1.4.3
.

Let
R:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
be a pseudo-full and faithful pseudofunctor with a bi-adjoint
L\dashv R
. If
\mathcal{B}
has bicolimits, then
\mathcal{A}
also has bicolimits which are computed as

{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}\,F\simeq L({\textup{bicolim}}^{W}\,RF)

Proof.

For
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
W:I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
, we have a pseudonatural equivalence in each
A
of
\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{A}[F,A]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[RF,R(A)]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}F,R(A)]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{A}[L({\textup{bicolim}}^{W}F),A]

∎

2
Notions of
2
-dimensional filteredness

This section deals with categorifications of the notion of filteredness: namely
bifilteredness
by
[
Ken92
]
and
[
Des20
]
and
\sigma
-filteredness
by
[
DDS18
]
, together with suited notion of 2-dimensional cofinality. Although those notions were developed separately, we show here they are actually almost the same in the sense that the seemingly more complicated
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits automatically collapse on ordinary bifiltered bicolimits, which was seemingly unnoticed before.

2.1
Bifilteredness and
\sigma
-filteredness

Definition 2.1.1

(
\sigma
-filtered pairs,
[
DDS18
]

\sigma
-filtered)
.

Let
I
be a 2-category and
\Sigma
a class of maps in
I
. Then
I
is said to be
\sigma
-filtered relatively to
\Sigma
, or that
(I,\Sigma)
is a
\sigma
-filtered pair
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

for any
i,\;i^{\prime}
in
I
there exists a span in
\Sigma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\in\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}\in\Sigma}

2.

for a parallel pair
d,s:i\rightrightarrows i^{\prime}
with
s
in
\Sigma
, there exists
t:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
in
\Sigma
and a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\in\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\in\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\in\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Moreover the 2-cell
\alpha
can be chosen to be invertible whenever
d
is also in
\Sigma
;

3.

for a pair of parallel 2-cells with codomain in
\Sigma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\in\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

there exists
f:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
also in
\Sigma
such that
f*\alpha=f*\alpha^{\prime}
.

In particular let us emphasize the following specific case into an autonomous definition:

Definition 2.1.2
.

A 2-category
I
is said to be
bifiltered
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

for any
i,\;i^{\prime}
in
I
there exists a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}

2.

for a parallel pair
d,d^{\prime}:i\rightrightarrows i^{\prime}
, there exists
f:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
together with an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

3.

for a pair of parallel 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

there exists
f:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
such that
f*\alpha=f*\alpha^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

Of course a
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
is bifiltered exactly when
\Sigma
contains all arrows.

Remark 2.1.4

(From finite to infinite filteredness)
.

Traditionally there is a gradient of notions of filteredness, indexed by a regular cardinals
\lambda
, so that the definition above should be called
\omega,\sigma
-filteredness. In this paper, we shall mainly stick to this
finite
notion, but in a couple of occurrences, we will need its infinite version. Thus, let us say that, in the notation of the definition above,
I
is said to be
\lambda,\sigma
-filtered if the condition (1) of the definition holds for any
\lambda
-small family of objects in
I
.

Remark 2.1.5

(Trivial cases)
.

Some reflections on the notion of
\sigma
-filteredness are needed. Let us analyze the trivial situations.

-

\Sigma
cannot be empty, unless
I
is biequivalent to the terminal
2
-category;

-

if the pair
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered, then the underlying category
I_{0}
of
I
is directed;

-

a filtered
1
-category can be equipped with a locally discrete
2
-dimensional structure such that the resulting
2
-category is
\sigma
-filtered for every choice of
\Sigma
.

Proposition 2.1.6

(
[
DDS18
, Proposition 3.1.5]
)
.

A
2
-category
I
is
\sigma
-filtered with respect to
\Sigma
if and only if any finite
2
-subcategory admits a
\sigma
-cone above it with arrows in
\Sigma
.

2.1.7
A concrete description of
\sigma
-bicolimits of in
{\bf Cat}
,
[
DDS16
, Def 2.1]
.

Here we should give a few words on
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-colimit of categories, which is a generalization of the construction of pseudocolimits from oplax colimits. The localization formula of
1.2.16
exhibits the
\sigma
-colimit as a category of fraction of the oplax colimit at the cartesian lifts of
\Sigma
-arrows, which can be shown to enjoy a right calculus of fractions. From
[
DDS16
, Definition 2.1]
, we can give the following more concrete description of
{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}_{i\in I}\,F(i)
when
(I,\Sigma)
is a
\sigma
-filtered pair: its objects are pairs
(i,a)
with
a
an object of
F(i)
, and a morphism
(i_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(i_{2},a_{2})
is the data of a span
s:i_{1}\rightarrow i_{3}
,
d:i_{2}\rightarrow i_{3}
in
I
with
s
in
\Sigma
and a morphism
\phi:F(s)(a_{1})\Rightarrow F(d)(a_{2})
, which can be visualized in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{s}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}}

In particular, we end with a composite arrow in the
\sigma
-bicolimit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{1}}F(s)(a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{3}}F(d)(a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{2}}(a_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{3}}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{d})_{a}}

which corresponds to the
(vertical, cocartesian)
factorization in the oplax colimit
\int F
.

Two 1-cells
(s,d,j,\phi),(s^{\prime},d^{\prime},j^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}):(i_{1},a_{1})\rightrightarrows(i_{2},a_{2})
of the oplax colimit are identified in the
\sigma
-bicolimit if one has the following equality of 2-cells (this relation being called
homotopies of premorphisms
in
[
DDS16
]
):

Remark 2.1.8
.

In particular, if
I
is a bifiltered 2-category, then a morphism in the bicolimit
{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{d^{\prime}}\atop\simeq}

with both the upper and lower inclusion 2-cells invertible, so that the resulting 2-cell
(i_{1},a_{1})\Rightarrow(i_{2},a_{2})
is obtained directly (up to invertible 2-cell) as the whiskering of
\phi
along
q_{i_{3}}
- differently from the general
\sigma
-filtered case where one has to paste this whiskering further with a non invertible lower inclusion 2-cell.

Remark 2.1.9

(
\sigma
-filtered diagrams are not too many)
.

\sigma
-filteredness puts actually some constrain on the relation between the shape of the category and the laxness of the 2-cells in the
\sigma
-colimit inclusion. While any oplax colimit is a case of
\sigma
-colimit with trivial choice of
\Sigma
(that is, containing only isomorphisms), being
\sigma
-filtered for such a choice of
\Sigma
trivializes the underlying 2-category as any two objects must be isomorphic. This prevents
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit to become too “wild”.

The following lemma, though being an easy consequence of the axioms of
\sigma
-filteredness, actually simplifies a lot the whole theory:

Lemma 2.1.10

(Triangle lemma)
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
be a
\sigma
-filtered pair. Then any arrow
d:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
I
can be completed into a triangle as below with
s,s^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Proof.

First choose a span
t:i\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
,
t^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
in
\Sigma
provided by the first axiom; one gets a parallel pair
t^{\prime}d,t:i\rightrightarrows i^{\prime\prime}
in
I
whose bottom arrow is in
\Sigma
. Hence there exists
t^{\prime\prime}:i^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow j
in
\Sigma
together with the desired 2-cell
\phi:t^{\prime\prime}t^{\prime}d\Rightarrow t^{\prime\prime}t
and both
t^{\prime\prime}t
and
t^{\prime\prime}t^{\prime}
are in
\Sigma
.
∎

2.1.11
.

A consequence of
Lemma

2.1.10
is that the non-invertible transition 2-cells in the
\sigma
-bicolimiting cocone under a
\sigma
-filtered diagram can always be “swallowed” by a member of the diagram: for any
d:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
, any choice of
s,s^{\prime},\phi
as above provides the following decomposition

Hence any non invertible transition 2-cell in the cocone can be replaced by a choice of a transition 2-cell in the diagram together with invertible transition 2-cells.

2.1.12
Morphisms in the
\sigma
-bicolimit comes from morphisms of the bicolimit.

In the case of a
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-colimit in
{\bf Cat}
, this means that cocartesian morphisms in the oplax colimit
\int F
acquire vertical mates when passing to the
\sigma
-bicolimit. Concretely, this means that the representation of morphisms in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
as given in
2.1.7
can be simplified as if we worked in an ordinarily bifiltered bicolimit: for a morphism
(i_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(i_{2},a_{2})
in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
represented by a triple
s:i_{1}\rightarrow i_{3}
,
d:i_{2}\rightarrow i_{3}
and
\phi:F(s)a_{1}\Rightarrow F(d)a_{2}
as above, the lower transition 2-cell
\theta_{d}
, which is by itself not invertible, can be replaced thanks to some choice
s_{2}:i_{2}\rightarrow i_{4}
,
s_{3}:i_{3}\rightarrow i_{4}
in
\Sigma
together with
\psi:s_{3}d\Rightarrow s_{2}
, which provides a decomposition of the diagram representing the morphism in the colimit as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{4})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{4}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{s}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{s_{3}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{s_{2}}\atop\simeq}

where all the colimiting transition 2-cells are invertible as indexed by morphisms in
\Sigma
. Moreover, any two such decompositions are identified in the
\sigma
-bicolimit under the relation described at
2.1.7
. This means that the corresponding arrow
(i_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(i_{2},a_{2})
in the
\sigma
-bicolimit comes in an essentially unique way from some arrow already existing in some member of the bicolimit.

The condition above is somewhat reminiscent of a condition of fullness: it says that any arrows in the
\sigma
-bicolimit comes from somewhere in the diagram. We should give here a complementary faithfulness statement.

Lemma 2.1.13
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
be a
\sigma
-filtered pair and
I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a pseudofunctor. For any parallel pair
(i,a)\rightrightarrows(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F

-

there exists a parallel pair of 2-cells as below, with
w,w^{\prime}
in
\Sigma

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(w^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}

providing representants of those two morphisms;

-

if moreover those 2-morphisms are identified in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
, then there exists
v:k\rightarrow l
in
\Sigma
such that
F(v)(\xi)=F(v)(\xi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We can pick two representants of this parallel pair, corresponding to 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

Now combining the first and second axioms of
\sigma
-filteredness one can find
u:j\rightarrow k
and
u^{\prime}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow k
together with 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}

which provides two alternative decompositions of the same morphism

\hbox to265.69pt{\vbox to123.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 132.8445pt\lower-61.6568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

We thus end with a parallel pair
in the same component

F(k)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi)*aF(u^{\prime})*\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi^{\prime})*a^{\prime}F(u)*\phi}

If now this parallel pair happens to be identified in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
(that is, coequified by whiskering with
q_{k})
: hence, applying
Remark

1.2.17
, there exists a further morphism
v:k\rightarrow l
in
\Sigma
such that
F(v)
coequifies this parallel pair

F(v)(F(\psi)*aF(u^{\prime})*\phi^{\prime})=F(v)(F(\psi^{\prime})*a^{\prime}F(u)*\phi)

∎

Remark 2.1.14
.

Although the last argument in
Lemma

2.1.13
relies on a general consideration about the calculus of fractions involved in arbitrary
\sigma
-bicolimits, the crucial simplifications used here are strictly specific to
\sigma
-filteredness and by no mean can be inferred to arbitrary
\sigma
-bicolimits.

2.2
2-dimensional cofinality and trivialization of
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit

A consequence of
Lemma

2.1.13
and
Lemma

2.1.10
is that
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits are actually no more complicated nor expressive than bifiltered bicolimits, in the sense that for a
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
, the arrows out of
\Sigma
are somewhat “useless” when it comes to compute an
I
-indexed
\sigma
-bicolimit relatively to
\Sigma
. This observation will be made formal thanks to the following notions:

Definition 2.2.1

(Cofinality)
.

Let
I
be a small 2-category with
\Sigma
a class of maps such that
I
is
\Sigma
-filtered,
J
another 2-category with
\Sigma^{\prime}
a class of maps in
J
and
F:I\rightarrow J
a pseudofunctor. Then
F
is
\sigma
-cofinal
with respect to
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
if

-

for any
j
in
J
there is some arrow
s:j\rightarrow F(i)
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
;

-

for any parallel pair
d,t:j\rightrightarrows F(i)
with
t
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
there is
s:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
and a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Moreover
\alpha
can be chosen to be invertible whenever
d
also is in
\Sigma
.

-

For any parallel 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

with
t
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, there exists
s:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
such that
F(s)*\alpha=F(s)*\alpha^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

This is a specific form of cofinality in the context of
\sigma
-filteredness, while a more general definition of cofinality may exist for non
\sigma
-filtered diagrams; however this one is sufficient for our purpose. Also we should point out that
[
DDS18
, Definition 3.3.1]
definition of
\sigma
-cofinality is given for a strict 2-functor, yet this does not modify anything in practice, for there is no relevant interaction between the data of
\sigma
-cofinality and the unit and composition data associated to the pseudofunctor. Moreover, for the classes of maps in
\sigma
-pairs can always be assumed to be closed under invertible 2-cells, one can check that a pseudofunctor admitting a strictification is
\sigma
-cofinal if and only if its strictification
\overline{F}
is so.

Lemma 2.2.3

(Cofinality preserve filteredness)
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
and
(J,\Sigma^{\prime})
be two
\sigma
-pairs and
F:I\rightarrow J
such that

-

I

\sigma
-filtered,

-

F
is
\sigma
-cofinal for
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
,

-

and
F(\Sigma)\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Then
(J,\Sigma^{\prime})
is
\sigma
-filtered.

Proof.

Let us prove the different conditions of
\sigma
-filteredness. First, for any
j,j^{\prime}
in
J
, there are, by cofinality, both
i,i^{\prime}
in
I
with
s:j\rightarrow i
and
s^{\prime}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
and then by
\Sigma
-filteredness of
I
a span
t:i\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
in
\Sigma
, and the composite
F(t)s
,
F(t^{\prime})s^{\prime}
are in
\Sigma^{\prime}
and provide a desired span. Now for a parallel pair
d,s:j\rightarrow F(i)
with
s
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, cofinalness entails the existence of
t:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
such that
F(t)
inserts some
\alpha:F(t)d\Rightarrow F(t)s
(which can be made invertible if
d
is in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, but
F(t)
is in
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Same argument for equalization of parallel 2-cells.
∎

We also have the following converse property:

Lemma 2.2.4

(
[
DDS18
]
Proposition 3.3.2)
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
and
(J,\Sigma^{\prime})
be two
\sigma
-pairs and
F:I\rightarrow J
a pseudofunctor such that

-

(J,\Sigma^{\prime})
is
\sigma
-filtered,

-

F
is pseudo-fully-faithful,

-

for each
j
in
J
there is some
s:i\rightarrow F(i)
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Then
F
is
\sigma
-cofinal for
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered.

The following proposition is a
\sigma
-version of
[
Des20
]
[Theorem 1.3.9] (which was originally stated for
bifilteredness
and
bicofinality
) confirms that
\sigma
-cofinal functors have the expected behavior regarding
\sigma
-colimits - at least for
\sigma
-filtered ones:

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
and
(J,\Sigma^{\prime})
be two
\sigma
-filtered pairs and
F:I\rightarrow J
a
\sigma
-cofinal pseudofunctor relatively to
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
F(\Sigma)\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
, and
G:J\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
a pseudofunctor. Then one has

\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,GF(i)

Proof.

For
F(\Sigma)\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
, the restriction of the colimiting
\sigma
-cocone for
G
to objects in the range of
F
defines a
\Sigma
-cocone
(q_{F(i)}:GF(i)\rightarrow{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}_{j\in J}G(j))_{i\in I}
as its 2-cells
q_{F(s)}
for
s
in
\Sigma
are invertible. Then we have a uniquely induced arrow
\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}
with a factorization of the colimit inclusion at each
i

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,GF(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GF(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

By cofinality, any
j
in
J
admits a
\Sigma^{\prime}
arrow
t:j\rightarrow F(i)
, so that we have a factorization of the corresponding
j
-inclusion through the following invertible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GF(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,GF(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{t}\atop\simeq}

Moreover such a factorization is actually uniquely defined: by cofinality, if one as two distinct arrows
t_{1}:j\rightarrow F(i_{1})
,
t_{2}:j\rightarrow F(i_{2})
in
\Sigma^{\prime}
, one has a
\Sigma
span
s_{1}:i_{1}\rightarrow i
,
s_{2}:i_{2}\rightarrow i
in
I
as
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered, and moreover one can choose this
i
together with an invertible 2-cell
\alpha:F(s_{1})t_{1}\simeq F(s_{2})t_{2}
. This ensures that one has actually a unique
g_{j}:G(j)\rightarrow{{\Sigma}{\textup{bicolim}}}_{i\in J}\,GF(i)
factorizing
q_{j}
through
\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}
: hence we have a canonical invertible 2-cell
1_{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}_{j\in J}G(j)}\simeq\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,GF(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Moreover one has a canonical invertible 2-cell
g_{j}\simeq\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}q_{j}
, so in particular one has
g_{F(i)}\simeq\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}q_{F(i)}
: but clearly
g_{F}(i)\simeq q^{\prime}_{i}
as one can take
1_{F(i)}
as the
t
in the construction of the
g_{F(i)}
above: this gives a converse factorization of the bicolimit inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GF(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{j\in J}{{\Sigma^{\prime}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,G(j)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,GF(i)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which in turn induces an invertible 2-cell

1_{{\Sigma}{\textup{bicolim}}_{i\in I}GF(i)}\simeq\langle g_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}\langle q_{F(i)}\rangle_{i\in I}

This exhibits the desired equivalence between those
\sigma
-bicolimits.
∎

Now, we come to a central observation, essential to the next sections of this work, which seems to have been unnoticed hitherto; yet it reduces the theory of
\sigma
-filteredness to the theory of bifilteredness thanks to an almost tautological argument of
\sigma
-cofinality:

Lemma 2.2.6

(Trivialization lemma)
.

Let
(I,\Sigma)
be a
\sigma
-pair. Then
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered if and only if the full on 0-cells and 2-cells subcategory
\Sigma\hookrightarrow I
(i.e., full on objects and
2
cells and containing only
\Sigma
as
1
-cells) is bifiltered and
\sigma
-cofinal relatively to
\Sigma
.

Proof.

This is just putting altogether the definition of
\sigma
-filteredness and
\sigma
-cofinality, having in mind that moreover a
\sigma
-pair
(I,\Sigma)
is bifiltered exactly when
\Sigma
contains all arrows of
I
. Although this is tautological, this lemma is important enough for us to state a carefull proof. Suppose that
(I,\Sigma)
is a
\sigma
-pair such that the subcategory
\Sigma
is bifiltered and the inclusion
\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow I
is
\sigma
-cofinal for
\Sigma
: then we are in the condition of
Lemma

2.2.3
, which entails that
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered. Conversely, suppose that
(I,\Sigma)
is
\sigma
-filtered. Then for
i,i^{\prime}
in
I
the span provided by
\sigma
-filteredness is in
\Sigma
. Now take a parallel pair
s,t:i\rightarrow j
with both
s,s^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
: then there exists
t
in
\Sigma
together with an invertible 2-cell
\alpha:ts\simeq ts^{\prime}
; similar argument for parallel pairs of 2-cells: the
\sigma
-filteredness of
(I,\Sigma)
automatically entails the bifilteredness of
\Sigma
.
∎

Remark 2.2.7
.

As a corollary, this proves that, any
\sigma
-filtered pair being actually controlled by the bifiltered subcategory made of its
\sigma
-filtration arrows, any
\sigma
-bicolimit over a
\sigma
-filtered pair is actually equivalent to the bicolimit over this
\sigma
-cofinal bifiltered subcategory. This will explain why the
\sigma
-bicolimit decomposition of pseudofunctors into
{\bf Cat}
can always be simplified to an ordinary bicolimit for flat pseudofunctors, and why
bifiltered bicolimits are sufficient in the definition of bi-accessible categories.

Corollary 2.2.8

(
\sigma
-filtered bicolimits are bifiltered bicolimits)
.

For any
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
, with
\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow I
being the corresponding inclusion, and any
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
one has an equivalence of categories

\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}F\simeq\underset{\Sigma}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F\iota_{\Sigma}

2.2.9
A direct proof without trivialization lemma.

It is worth the detailing of the construction to convince oneself of the fact above, for it might sound surprisingly strong. For this reason, we choose to provide here a concrete proof of the corollary above without invoking the trivialization lemma. Indeed, one could ask how the data encoded in the morphisms out of
\Sigma
are managed in the restricted filtered bicolimit over
\Sigma
if it is the same as the
\sigma
-bicolimit, knowing that the cocartesian morphisms are all invertible in the restricted bicolimit while those indexed by morphisms out of
\Sigma
are not in the
\sigma
-bicolimit. Let us examine the case of the
\sigma
-bicolimit of a diagram of small categories
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
over a
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
. Denote
\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\rightarrow I
the corresponding inclusion: we have an induced functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{\Sigma}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F\iota_{\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

induced by the inclusions
q_{i}
over the restricted cocone over
\Sigma
. Let us prove that this functor is essentially surjective on objects, full and faithful by using successively the different axioms of cofinality. Essential surjectivity makes no doubt as
\iota_{\Sigma}
is essentially surjective on objects. Now take a morphism
(i,a)\rightarrow(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
: it can be presented by data
s:i\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime},d:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
and
\phi:F(s)a\Rightarrow F(d)a^{\prime}
with
s
in
\Sigma
as in
2.1.7
, but from
2.1.12
this presentation can be itself replaced by a better presentation where
d
is substituted with a 2-cell
\psi:td\Rightarrow t^{\prime}
with both
t,t^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
: then the following pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(ts)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi)*a^{\prime}F(t)*\phi}

can be used to represent a morphism
(i,a)\rightarrow(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in the restricted bicolimit
{\textup{bicolim}}_{\Sigma}F\iota_{\Sigma}
which returns the original morphism in
\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
through the following whiskering, proving
q
to be full:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}_{\Sigma}F\iota_{\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(ts)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\psi)*a^{\prime}F(t)*\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{ts}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{t^{\prime}}\atop\simeq}

Finally, to prove
q
to be faithful: suppose we have two morphisms
(i,a)\rightrightarrows(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in the bicolimit that are identified in the
\Sigma
-bicolimit after whiskering with
q
. Then by
Lemma

2.1.13
, there is a parallel pair of 2-cells as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}

such that
(s,s^{\prime},\phi)
and
(s,s^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
provides representants for the two morphisms above in
{\textup{bicolim}}_{\Sigma}F\iota_{\Sigma}
; but now, they are moreover coequified by
q_{k}:F(k)\rightarrow\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}_{I}F
by hypothesis. Hence by
Lemma

2.1.13
there is a
t:k\rightarrow l
in
\Sigma
such that
F(t)(\phi)=F(t)(\phi^{\prime})
. But this implies in particular that
(s,s^{\prime},\phi)
and
(s,s^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
are already identified in the bicolimit
{\textup{bicolim}}_{\Sigma}F\iota_{\Sigma}
. Hence
q
is faithful. This achieves to prove the equivalence of categories between the
\sigma
-bicolimit and the corresponding restricted bicolimit, and we hope at least the reader is convinced.

Corollary 2.2.10
.

A 2-category
\mathcal{C}
has bifiltered bicolimits if and only if it has
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits. Moreover a pseudofunctor preserves bifiltered bicolimits if and only if it preserves
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits.

Proof.

It is clear that cocompleteness under
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits entails cocompleteness under bifiltered bicolimits as the latter are instances of the first; same argument for preservation. But now, suppose one has a
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Then for
\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow I
is
\sigma
-cofinal relatively to
\Sigma
and
\Sigma
is bifiltered, we can compute the restricted bifiltered bicolimit
{\textup{bicolim}}F\iota_{\Sigma}
in
\mathcal{C}
, and it provides a
\sigma
-bicolimit over
I
. Now if
G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
preserves bifiltered bicolimits:
∎

We should end this section by recalling the following crucial property, which can be found at
[
DDS18
]
[2.7.3] and also
[
DDS16
]
[Theorem 3.2] - again, it is stated there for 2-functors yet it works for pseudofunctors:

Proposition 2.2.11
.

\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-colimits commute with finitely weighted bilimits in
{\bf Cat}
: for
(I,\Sigma)
a
\sigma
-filtered pair and
J
a finite category with
W:J\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a finite weight, if
F:I\times J\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
is a pseudofunctor, then one has

\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bilim}}^{W}}\,F(i,j)\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bilim}}^{W}}\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i,j)

In particular, bifiltered bicolimits commute with finitely weighted bilimits in
{\bf Cat}
.

3
Bi-accessible and bipresentable 2-categories

This core section introduces 2-dimensional analogs of accessible and locally presentable categories. Our notions are closely related to
[
Kel82
]
definition, up to the difference that the latter is stated in an enriched context with strict constructions, while ours is suited for a weaker context; in particular, while
[
Kel82
]
directly use 1-dimensional filtered colimit in 2-categories together with strict 2-limits or colimits, we use bifiltered bicolimits for our definition of presentability.

As opposed to the very 1-dimensional and strict version of
[
Kel82
]
, we build here a
2
-dimensional theory of accessibility from the more genuinely 2-dimensional approach of
[
DDS18
]
in order to connect our definition with their theory of flat pseudofunctors. However, we should emphasize the fact that, though this later formalism relies on
\sigma
-filteredness, we can foresee from
Lemma

2.2.6
that we can reduce to only consider bifiltered bicolimits, as we will see in several instances as
Proposition

3.1.8
and
Lemma

3.2.5
.

3.1
Bicompact objects

This subsection is devoted to the properties of our 2-dimensional analog of finitely presented objects
[
AR94
]
- also known as
compact
objects, a terminology we prefer in order to avoid confusion with bipresentability - for we consider
bicompact categories
we will avoid to confuse with presentable categories in the ordinary sense. As well as ordinary compact objects, bicompact objects will be defined through a lifting property relatively to bifiltered bicolimits.

Definition 3.1.1

(Bicompactness)
.

An object
K
in a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is said to be (finitely)
bicompact
if for any bifiltered 2-category
I
and any pseudofunctor
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
, the functor induced from composing with bicolimits inclusions provides us with an equivalence of categories

\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]\simeq\mathcal{B}[K,{\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F}]

Remark 3.1.2
.

There is no difference with defining the lifting property of the bicompact against 2-functors rather than pseudofunctors: one can check that any object having the property above against 2-functors satisfies it automatically for pseudofunctors. This is because the conditions in bicompactness do not interact in a particular manner with unit and composition of pseudofunctors.

Remark 3.1.3
.

As in
Remark

2.1.4
, we can define
\lambda,\sigma
-compactness by preservation of
\lambda
-bifiltered bicolimits.

3.1.4
A concrete description of bicompactness:
1
-cells.

Unravelling this definition gives us the explicit definition of a bicompact object. For any
a:K\rightarrow{\textup{bicolim}}_{i\in I}F(i)
we can pick some
b:K\rightarrow F(i)
and an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\atop\simeq}

and moreover, any such two choices
(b,\beta)
and
(b^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of lifts over
a
must be isomorphic in the bicolimit, which means that there is some
i^{\prime\prime}\in I
and
d:i\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
and
d^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime}
together with an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\atop\simeq}

whose pasting with the canonical 2-cells of the bicolimit provides with a 2-cell

\hbox to246.9pt{\vbox to88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 123.4475pt\lower-43.99706pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

3.1.5
A concrete description of bicompactness: 2-cells.

Moreover, for any 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

it suffices to paste it with the invertible 2-cells at two lifts as below to get a morphism between lifts
