Lemma 3.1.6

(Lifts of parallel 2-cells)
.

Similarly, one can lift parallels pairs of 2-cells into a parallel pair between the sames lifts: for any parallel pair of 2-cells of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

there exists a span
d:i\rightarrow j
,
d^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow j
together with
b:K\rightarrow F(i)
,
b^{\prime}:K\rightarrow F(i^{\prime})
and invertible 2-cells
\beta:a\simeq F(d)b
,
\beta^{\prime}:a^{\prime}\simeq F(d^{\prime})b^{\prime}
, and parallel 2-cells
\zeta,\xi:F(d)b\Rightarrow F(d^{\prime})b^{\prime}
such that
q_{d}^{-1}q_{j}*\zeta q_{d^{\prime}}=\beta^{\prime}\phi\beta^{-1}
and
q_{d}^{-1}q_{j}*\xi q_{d^{\prime}}=\beta^{\prime}\psi\beta^{-1}
.

Proof.

These
\zeta
and
\xi
can be constructed as follows: take lifts
(b_{\phi},b_{\phi}^{\prime},i_{\phi},i_{\phi}^{\prime},j_{\phi},d_{\phi},d^{\prime}_{\phi},\alpha_{\phi})
and
(b_{\psi},b_{\psi}^{\prime},i_{\psi},i_{\psi}^{\prime},j_{\psi},d_{\psi},d^{\prime}_{\psi},\alpha_{\psi})
as in
3.1.5
: then one has a common refinement
t_{\phi}:j_{\phi}\rightarrow j
,
t_{\psi}:j_{\psi}\rightarrow j
, and moreover this common refinement can be chosen such that there is also invertible 2-cells
\gamma^{\prime}:F(t_{\phi})F(d^{\prime}_{\phi})b^{\prime}_{\phi}\simeq F(t_{\psi})F(d^{\prime}_{\psi})b^{\prime}_{\psi}
, which altogether provides the following two parallel composites 2-cells one can choose as the desired
\zeta,\xi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{\phi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{\phi}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j_{\phi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime}_{\psi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j_{\psi})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{\psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime}_{\psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime}_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{\phi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j_{\phi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{\psi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j_{\psi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime}_{\psi})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{\psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime}_{\psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d_{\psi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\psi}}

∎

Remark 3.1.7
.

One could also be tempted to define a notion of
\sigma
-compact
objects as having the analogous property relatively to
\sigma
-filtered colimits. From
Corollary

2.2.8
such a notion would be redundant:

Proposition 3.1.8
.

Bicompact have the lifting property relative to
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits: an object
K
of a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is bicompact if and only if for any
\sigma
-filtered pair
(I,\Sigma)
and any
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
one has

\mathcal{B}[K,{\underset{I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F}]\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]

Proof.

It is clear that such a property implies bicompactness; if now
K
is bicompact, then combining
Corollary

2.2.8
with
Lemma

2.2.6
one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[K,{\underset{I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F}]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[K,{\underset{\Sigma}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F\iota_{\Sigma}}]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in\Sigma}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]

∎

Remark 3.1.9

(A comparison with the literature)
.

Two notions of finitely presentable categories had historically been introduced:

-

In
[
Str76
]
, they are defined via the notion of computad, see
[
Str76
, between Thm. 3 and Prop. 4]
. Notably, finite categories are finitely presentable in the sense of Street, and they are also in our sense (see
Lemma

3.1.10
). It would be very interesting to have a similar presentation of our notion of compactness, especially in the direction of providing a characterization as in
[
Str76
, Prop. 4]

-

In
[
Kel82
]
, Kelly defined finitely presentable categories in terms of filtered colimits, to be precise, as to this source an object
X
is finitely presentable if the functor
{\bf Cat}[X,-]
preserves
conical
filtered colimits (see
[
Kel82
, 1.1 and 2.1]
). This notion is too strict to be compared with ours, but its bi-version would be comparable, because of
Remark

2.1.5
.

Lemma 3.1.10
.

In
{\bf Cat}
, finite categories are bicompact.

Proof.

For any finite category
C
and
F:I\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
(which can be chosen as a strict 2-functor) with
I
bifiltered, a functor
G:I\rightarrow{\textup{pscolim}}\,F
can be described as picking for each
c\in C
an object
(i,x)
with
i\in I
and
x\in F(i)
in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}\,F
such that any two such choices for the same
c
have to live in the same isomorphism class in the localization
{\textup{pscolim}}\,F=\Pi_{1}{\textup{oplaxcolim}}\,F[{\bf Cart}_{F}^{-1}]
; but as
C
is finite, if one picks
i_{c}
for each
c
in
\mathcal{C}
there exists some
i_{0}
and a family of arrows
d_{c}:i_{c}\rightarrow i_{0}
in
I
. Moreover, for any
g:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we can pick a representant
(f_{g},\phi_{g})
with
f_{g}:i_{c}\rightarrow i_{c^{\prime}}
and
\phi_{g}:F(f)(x_{c})\rightarrow x_{c^{\prime}}
for a convenient choice of
(i_{c},x_{c})
and
(i_{c^{\prime}},x_{c^{\prime}})
. Then one can define an assignment
H:C\rightarrow F(i_{0})
sending
c
on
(i_{0},F(d_{c})(x_{c}))
. In fact
H
may not yet be a functor, but one can use bifilteredness of
I
to correct it into a functor: for a map
g:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
with a representant
(f_{g},\phi_{g})
we have in
i_{0}
a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(d_{c^{\prime}})F(f_{g})(x_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(d_{c^{\prime}})(x_{c^{\prime}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g})}

Now we have a parallel pair in
{\textup{oplaxcolim}}\,F

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{c},x_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{c^{\prime}},F(f_{g})(x_{c}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},F(d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{c},F(d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{g},1_{F(f_{g})(x_{c})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{c^{\prime}},1_{F(d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})})}

with the composite
(d_{c^{\prime}},1_{F(d_{c^{\prime}})F(f_{g})(x_{c})})(f_{g},1_{F(f_{g})(x_{c})})
cocartesian. Hence there exists a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{c},x_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{c^{\prime}},F(f_{g})(x_{c}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{0},F(d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{c},F(d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{g},1_{F(f_{g})(x_{c})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{c^{\prime}},1_{F(d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1},1_{F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}},1_{F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}d_{c},F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

which provides an intermediate map

F(f_{1}d_{c})(x_{c})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})

one can compose with
F(d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g})
to get a map as desired

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}}f_{g})(x_{c})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(f_{1}d_{c^{\prime}})(x_{c^{\prime}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(f_{1}d_{c})(x_{c})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d_{c^{\prime}})(\phi_{g})}

Hence it is mostly a technical effort to show that this defines an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{pscolim}}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

showing the bicompactness of
C
.
∎

Remark 3.1.11
.

[Bicompact categories might not be finite]
We do not assert that all bicompact objects in
{\bf Cat}
are finite. For example, the monoid
\mathbb{N}
- seen as a
1
-object category - is the coinserter of the diagram below and thus is bicompact, being a finite bicolimit of bicompact objects by
Proposition

3.1.12
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{*\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{*\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}}}
id
id

Bicompactness behaves as nicely as it can. We will now list some theorems that show how the theory of bicompact objects perfectly mirrors that of compact objects in
[
AR94
]
. The following two results below deal with the closure properties of bicompact objects; the first one is the
2
-dimensional analog of
[
AR94
, 1.3]
.

Proposition 3.1.12

(Bicompact objects are closed under finite weighed bicolimits)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a 2-category,
W:I^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
a finite weight and
G:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
a pseudofunctor such that each
G(i)
is bicompact. Then
{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}\,G
still is bicompact.

Proof.

Let
F:J\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
be a pseudofunctor with
J
bifiltered. We have an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}^{W}\;G,\underset{J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[G,\underset{J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;F]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[G,F(j)]\big]

where the last equivalence comes from the fact that each
G(i)
is bicompact. But now for any pseudonatural transformation
\phi:W\Rightarrow{{\textup{bicolim}}}_{j\in J}\,\mathcal{B}[G,F(j)]
, each
W(i)
is a finite category, hence by
Lemma

3.1.10
, is bicompact in
{\bf Cat}
, so we have a pseudonatural equivalence

{\bf Cat}\big[W(i),\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[G(i),F(j)]\big]\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,{\bf Cat}\big[W(i),\mathcal{B}[G(i),F(j)]\big]

Now as pointwise natural equivalences are equivalences in
{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
this means we have an equivalence in
{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
, which achieves to prove that
{\textup{bicolim}}^{W}\,G
is bicompact in
\mathcal{B}
.

\displaystyle{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[G,F(j)]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,{\bf ps}[I^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[W,\mathcal{B}[G,F(j)]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{j\in J}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[\underset{I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}^{W}\,G,F(j)]

∎

Definition 3.1.13

(Pseudoretracts)
.

We recall that a
pseudoretract
of an object
A
is an object
B
equipped with an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

Corollary 3.1.14
.

A pseudoretract of a bicompact is bicompact.

Now we get back briefly to
\sigma
-bicolimits which are involved in the following auxiliary notion, we shall make use of when embedding bi-accessible 2-categories in 2-categories of pseudofunctors.

Definition 3.1.15

(
\sigma
-tiny)
.

An object
K
in a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is said to be
\sigma
-tiny
if for any
\sigma
-pair
(I,\Sigma)
and
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
, we have an equivalence of category

\mathcal{B}[K,{\underset{I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F}]\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\Sigma{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]

Remark 3.1.16
.

In other words, a
\sigma
-tiny object preserves any small
\sigma
-bicolimits. Any
\sigma
-tiny object is in particular bicompact. As
\sigma
-tiny are tiny, the only
\sigma
-tiny object in
{\bf Cat}
is the point category.

3.2
Bi-accessible 2-categories

Definition 3.2.1

(Bi-accessible
2
-category)
.

A 2-category
\mathcal{B}
will be said
finitely bi-accessible
if

-

\mathcal{B}
has bifiltered bicolimits,

-

there is an essentially small full on
1
-cells and
2
-cells sub-2-category
\mathcal{B}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
consisting of bicompact objects such that for any
B
in
\mathcal{B}
is a bifiltered bicolimit of object in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

We refer to such a choice of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as
a generator of bicompact objects
. As in
Remark

3.1.3
and
Remark

2.1.4
, a
\lambda
-bi-accessible
2
-category can be defined as above by replacing all the occurrences of compactness and filteredness with their
\lambda
-generalization.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, then the full on
1
-cells and
2
-cells sub-2-category consisting of all bicompact objects is essentially small.

Proof.

Consider a bicompact object
X
. By definition of accessibility, this a bifiltered bicolimit of objects in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, as above. Thus, the identity
X\to X
has to factor through the bifiltered bicolimit. As a result, we obtain that
X
is the pseudoequalizer of a pair of parallel morphisms on a bicompact object in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Hence bicompact objects form a set up to isomorphisms.
∎

3.2.3
The canonical
\sigma
-cocone and the bidenseness condition.

If
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, then one can consider the
canonical lax-cone
of
B
relatively to the full subcategory of all bicompact objects
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
which is defined by the
binerve

\nu_{B}
of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
at
B
, which is the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}^{{\rm op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{{\rm op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}[-,B]}

This defines a fibration
\int\nu_{B}
over
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
whose objects are pairs
(K,a)
with
a:K\rightarrow B
, and a morphism
(K_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(K_{2},a_{2})
is a pair
(k,\phi)
coding for a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

In particular, cartesian morphisms are exactly those
(k,\phi)
where
\phi
is an isomorphism
a_{1}\simeq a_{2}k
. They will be denoted as
{\bf Cart}_{B}
. Observe that
\int\nu_{B}
is equivalent to the oplax-slice
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
.

3.2.4
The canonical pseudococone.

Although the canonical
\sigma
-cocone
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
contains all the necessary information about
B
relatively to
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
, we are going to see below that this information is actually condensed into a canonical
pseudococone
consisting of the restriction to cartesian morphisms over
B
, which corresponds to the restriction of the pseudoslice over
B
to the bicompact objects
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
.

Lemma 3.2.5

(The canonical pseudococone is bifiltered)
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible with
\mathcal{B}_{0}
a generator of bicompact objects, then for any object
B
, if
F:I_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{0}
with
I_{B}
bifiltered is such that
B\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}\,F
, then the inclusion

I_{B}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B

sending
i
on
q_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow B
is bicofinal, whence
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
is bifiltered and
B\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
.

Proof.

By assumption of finite bi-accessibility, one has for each
B
a
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit decomposition

B\simeq\underset{i\in I_{B}}{{\Sigma_{B}}{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)

with the
F:I_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
returning bicompact objects. In particular this diagram admits an embedding into the pseudoslice of bicompact objects over
B

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B}}

sending
i
on the pair
(F(i),q_{i})
,
d:i\rightarrow j
on the invertible 2-cell
(F(d),q_{d}):(F(i),q_{i})\rightarrow(F(j),q_{j})
and the 2-cells as expected. We must show this embedding to be bicofinal to deduce bifilteredness of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
from the bifilteredness of
I_{B}
.

For
K
in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
any
a:K\rightarrow B
factorizes through some
q_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow B
, so we have a triangle in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\atop\simeq}

This is in particular the name of a cartesian arrow
(b,\sigma):(K,a)\rightarrow(F_{i},q_{i})
in . This ensures the first condition of cofinality.

For the second condition, suppose one has a parallel pair
(b,\beta),(b^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}):(K,a)\rightrightarrows(F(i),q_{i})
in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
: then this provides two invertible 2-cells
\beta:a\simeq q_{i}b
and
\beta^{\prime}:a\simeq q_{i}b^{\prime}
, so that
(b,\beta),(b^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are two lifts of the same arrow, so by
3.1.4
one has a span
d:i\rightarrow j
,
d^{\prime}:i\rightarrow j
in
I_{B}
together with some invertible two-cell
\gamma
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d^{\prime}}\atop\simeq}

As the lifts
b,b^{\prime}
were through the same index
i
, the span provided by bicompactness is actually a parallel pair in
I_{B}
: hence by bifilteredness there exists a further arrow
d^{\prime\prime}:j\rightarrow k
in
I_{B}
together with an invertible 2-cell
\alpha:d^{\prime\prime}d\simeq d^{\prime\prime}d^{\prime}
: then the composite
d^{\prime\prime}d:i\rightarrow k
together with the pasting
\alpha d^{\prime\prime}\gamma
provides the desired inserted 2-cell.

To conclude, if now one has two parallel 2-cells
\beta,\beta^{\prime}:(b,\sigma)\Rightarrow(b^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
(with both
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
invertible), that is two simultaneous factorizations of
\sigma:q_{i}b\Rightarrow a
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

expressing the equalities
\sigma^{\prime}q_{i}*\beta=\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}q_{i}*\beta^{\prime}
, then for
K
is bicompact we have common lift
(j,j^{\prime},s,d^{\prime},k,\psi)
together with invertible 2-cells
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
as below providing a decomposition of
\sigma\sigma^{\prime-1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\atop\simeq}

But then, combining bicompactness of
K
and bifilteredness of
I_{B}
, one can find a
l
in
I_{B}
together with
t:i\rightarrow l
and
r:k\rightarrow l
together with decompositions of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
as pasting of invertible 2-cells
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
such that this same 2-cell factorizes as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(l)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(j^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{t}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{t}\atop\simeq}

Then this exactly says that
F(t)
coequalizes
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
into the same invertible 2-cell
\delta^{\prime-1}F(r)*\psi\delta:F(t)b\simeq F(t)b^{\prime}
, which manifests itself as a coequalizing 2-cell in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(i),q_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(l),q_{l})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b,\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F(t),\theta_{t})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}

This achieves to prove the bicofinalness of the inclusion of
I_{B}
in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B

∎

Corollary 3.2.6
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, then for any
B
the canonical pseudocone
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
is bifiltered.

Proof.

Combine the lemma above with
Lemma

2.2.3
.
∎

Remark 3.2.7
.

As a consequence, we know also from
Lemma

2.2.6
that the oplax cocone
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
is
\sigma
-filtered for
{\bf Cart}_{B}
, as the pseudococone coincides with the restriction of the oplax cocone to cartesian arrows. Moreover by
\sigma
-cofinality, we also have a
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit decomposition
B\simeq{\bf Cart}_{B}{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
.

As in the
1
-dimensional case, we can provide an equivalent characterization of accessibility, providing a canonical candidate for the choice of the generator in an accessible category. The corollary below is the
2
-dimensional analog of
[
MP89
, page 19]
.

Corollary 3.2.8
.

A 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible if and only if

-

\mathcal{B}
has bifiltered bicolimits

-

the full subcategory
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
of bicompact objects is essentially small

-

for any object
B
the canonical pseudococone provides an equivalence

B\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B

Moreover, from what precedes, we know this characterization can be rephrased in term of
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits - observing that cocompleteness under bifiltered bicolimits is sufficient to entail cocompleteness under
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit as stated in
Corollary

2.2.10
:

Corollary 3.2.9
.

A 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible if and only if

-

\mathcal{B}
has
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits

-

the full subcategory
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
of bicompact objects is essentially small

-

for any object
B
, the oplax cocone together with the class of cartesian arrows provides us with a
\sigma
-bicolimit:

B\simeq{\bf Cart}_{B}{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B

This latter characterization, though seemingly redundant given the previous one, is of interest because it more directly relates with the notion of
bidenseness
which is a condition on the
binerve
.

Definition 3.2.10
.

For
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
a pseudofunctor with
\mathcal{C}
a small 2-category, the
binerve
of
F
is the pseudofunctor
\nu_{F}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow{\bf ps}[\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
sending
D
on the pseudofunctor
[F,D]:\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
. A pseudofunctor
F
is said to be
bidense
if its binerve
\nu_{F}
is pseudo-fully-faithful.

As expected, bidenseness of a functor equates to the possibility to decompose any object as a bicolimit over a convenient notion of cocone from the functor to this object; while this bicolimit decomposition is usually stated in term of weights, it can be de-weighted into a
\sigma
-bicolimit:

Lemma 3.2.11
.

For a small full sub-2-category
\mathcal{C}
of a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
, the following are equivalent:

-

Any object
B
in
\mathcal{B}
decomposes as the
\sigma
-bicolimit:
B\simeq{\Sigma_{B}}{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{C}\Downarrow B
for the
\sigma
-pair
(\mathcal{C}\Downarrow B,\Sigma_{B})
where
\Sigma_{B}
consists of all oplax cells above
B
whose underlying 2-cell is invertible;

-

The binerve 2-functor
\nu_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow{\bf ps}[\mathcal{C}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
is pseudo-fully faithful, that is,
\mathcal{C}
is a bidense subcategory of
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

The binerve functor sends
B
on the pseudofunctor
\mathcal{B}[\iota_{\mathcal{C}},B]
where
\iota_{\mathcal{C}}
is the full inclusion of
\mathcal{C}
. In one direction, for
B_{1},B_{2}
, a pseudonatural transformation
\phi:\mathcal{B}[\iota_{\mathcal{C}},B_{1}]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}[\iota_{\mathcal{C}},B_{2}]
defines uniquely a
\sigma
-cocone indexed by the
\sigma
-pair
(\mathcal{C}\Downarrow B_{2},\Sigma_{B})
as follows: take a pair
(C,a)
with
a:C\rightarrow B_{1}
to
\phi_{C}(a)
and a 2-cell
(u,\alpha):(C_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2},a_{2})
to the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{C_{2}}(a_{2})u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{C_{1}}(a_{2}u)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{C_{1}}(a_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{u}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{C_{1}}(\alpha)}

where
\phi_{u}
is invertible pseudonaturality component of
\phi
at
u
. This 2-cell is invertible whenever
\alpha
is, that is, whenever
(u,\alpha)
is in
\Sigma_{B_{1}}
. This defines a
\sigma
-cocone over
B_{2}
, and
B_{1}\simeq\sigma_{\Sigma_{B_{1}}}\!{\textup{bicolim}}\mathcal{C}\Downarrow B_{1}
ensures that we end up with a unique 1-cell
\phi:B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
in
\mathcal{B}
; this moreover can be done functorially relative to natural modifications
\phi\Rrightarrow\psi
, which we let as an exercise. The converse direction is obvious.
∎

Remark 3.2.12
.

In particular, it follows from the previous lemma and the discussion above that for any bi-accessible 2-category
\mathcal{B}
, the pseudofully faithful inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is bidense, which amounts to saying that the corresponding binerve sending any
B
on the strict 2-functor
\mathcal{B}[\iota,B]
, is a pseudo-fully faithful pseudofunctor,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

But the point of this observation is less to provide a further restatement of the definition of bi-accessibility in term of bidenseness than leading us to consider the binerve relative to bicompact objects. Instead, as in the 1-dimensional case, crucial properties of bi-accessible (and later bipresentable) 2-categories will be retrieved from the possibility to embed them into the category of pseudofunctors over their bicompact objects thanks to this binerve.

Lemma 3.2.13
.

The binerve pseudofunctor
\nu:\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
preserves bifiltered bicolimits (and thus
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimits).

Proof.

Take
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
with
I
bifiltered. In each bicompact object
K
and we have an equivalence

\mathcal{B}[\iota(K),{\textup{bicolim}}\;F]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{K}}}{{\simeq}}\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[\iota(K),F(i)]

We must show this equivalence is pseudonatural in
K
. Take
f:K_{1}\rightarrow K_{2}
in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
: we must prove that any precomposition by
f
of a lift is isomorphic to a lift of the precomposition by
f
. For any
a:K_{2}\rightarrow{\textup{bicolim}}\,F
there is for some
i\in I
an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\atop\simeq}

Then whiskering with
f
produces a lift
(xf,\xi*f)
of
af
. Now take another lift
(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
of
af

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces af}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}\atop\simeq}

Since
K_{1}
is bicompact and
I
is bifiltered, there exists
d:i\rightarrow j
and
d^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow j
and an invertible 2-cell
\gamma
such that

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -26.15 -26.8 h 14.31 v 16.81 h -14.31 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

This means that
xf
and
x^{\prime}
are isomorphic in
{{\textup{bicolim}}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]
. Denote as
(e_{f})_{a}
this isomorphism. Then we claim this produces a pseudocommutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}[\iota(K_{2}),{\textup{bicolim}}\;F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[\iota(K_{2}),F(i)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}[\iota(K_{1}),{\textup{bicolim}}\;F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[\iota(K_{1}),F(i)]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}[f,{\textup{bicolim}}\;F]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{K_{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;[f,F(i)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{K_{1}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}\atop\simeq}

Now consider
\phi:a_{1}\Rightarrow a_{2}
in
\mathcal{B}[K_{2},{\textup{bicolim}}F]
. It lifts as a 2-cell
\psi:F(d_{1})x_{1}\Rightarrow F(d_{2})x_{2}
with
d_{1}:i_{1}\rightarrow j
and
d_{2}:i_{2}\rightarrow j
with
(x_{1},\xi_{1})
cartesian and
(x_{2},\xi_{2})
lifts of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
respectively, and whiskering with
f
provides lift of
f^{*}\phi
; now if we choose an alternative lift
\psi^{\prime}:F(i_{1}^{\prime})x_{1}^{\prime}\Rightarrow F(i_{2}^{\prime})x_{2}^{\prime}
of
f*\phi
, we know that there are isomorphisms
(e^{f})_{a_{1}}:x_{1}f\simeq x_{1}^{\prime}
and
(e^{f})_{a_{2}}:x_{2}f\simeq x_{2}^{\prime}
in
{{\textup{bicolim}}}_{i\in I}\mathcal{B}[K,F(i)]
, and we have a commutation of 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{1}}x_{1}f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{1}^{\prime}}x_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{2}}x_{2}f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{i_{2}^{\prime}}x_{2}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi*f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e_{f})_{a_{1}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e_{f})_{a_{2}}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}

which expresses the naturality of
e_{f}
. Finally, hence the
e_{f}
, for
f
ranging over all 1-cell between bicompact objects, altogether define a pseudonatural equivalence

\mathcal{B}[\iota,{\textup{bicolim}}\;F]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\simeq}}\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;\mathcal{B}[\iota,F(i)]

∎

We are going to see later (
Theorem

4.1.7
) that this binerve pseudofunctor identifies any bi-accessible 2-category with the 2-category of
flat pseudofunctors
in the sense of
[
DDS18
]
over its generator of bicompact objects. But we defer the study of flatness to a further section, and turn now to bipresentable 2-categories where further properties will be extracted from the binerve.

3.3
Bipresentable 2-categories

Definition 3.3.1

(Bipresentable 2-categories)
.

A 2-category is said to be
finitely bipresentable
if it is finitely bi-accessible and has all small weighted bicolimits. We define
\lambda
-bipresentable
2
-categories as
\lambda
-bi-accessible
2
-categories with all small weighted bicolimits.

It is worth detailing how one can see directly in the case of a finitely bipresentable 2-category why the canonical diagram
(\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B,{\bf Cart}_{B})
is a
\sigma
-filtered pair and, for this very reason, the pseudoslice
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
is lax-cofinal in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
relatively to cartesian morphisms. From
Proposition

3.1.12
, we know that finitely weighted bicolimits of bicompacts are bicompacts. This encompasses in particular bicoproducts, bicoinserter and bicoequifiers. Altogether, these arguments will ensure that the pair made of the oplax-slice together with is cartesian morphisms
(\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B,{\bf Cart}_{B})
is
\sigma
-filtered - from which we automatically deduce bifilteredness and cofinality of the pseudoslice.

3.3.2
Discrete cones via bicoproducts.

Hence in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
if one has two objects
(K_{1},a_{1})
and
(K_{2},a_{2})
, then there is a common factorization through the bicoproduct

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{1}+K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}\atop\simeq}

and from the universal property of the bicolimit, one has invertible 2-cells
\alpha_{1}:a_{1}\simeq\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle q_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}:a_{2}\simeq\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle q_{2}
exhibiting the pairs
(q_{1},\alpha_{1}):(K_{1},a_{1})\rightarrow(K_{1}+K_{2},\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)
,
(q_{2},\alpha_{2}):(K_{2},a_{2})\rightarrow(K_{1}+K_{2},\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle)
as a span of cartesian arrows in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
.

3.3.3
Insertion of 2-cells.

For a parallel pair
(k_{1},\phi),(k_{2},\sigma):(K_{1},a_{1})\rightrightarrows(K_{2},a_{2})
with
(k_{2},\sigma)
cartesian, that is with
\sigma:a_{2}k_{2}\simeq a_{1}
invertible, then
a_{2}
inserts a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{-1}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Hence we have a factorizations of
a_{2}
through the bicoinserter of
k_{1},k_{2}
(which is bicompact):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{coIns}(k_{1},k_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\sigma^{-1}\phi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(k_{1},k_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

which provides in particular a cartesian 1-cell in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
. This inserts a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{2},a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\textbf{coIns}(k_{1},k_{2}),\langle\sigma^{-1}\phi\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{2},a_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{1},\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{(k_{1},k_{2})},\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{2},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{(k_{1},k_{2})},\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{(k_{1},k_{2})}}

where
\phi_{(k_{1},k_{2})}
is the universal 2-cell inserted by the bicoinserter.

3.3.4
Equification of parallel 2-cells.

If now one has parallel 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{2},a_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{1},\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{2},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}

then one has an equality of 2-cell in the oplax slice

\hbox to139.31pt{\vbox to65.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 69.65442pt\lower-23.90279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.35277pt}{21.7111pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Hence
a_{2}
coequifies
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, hence we have as above a factorizations through the bicoequifier (which is bicompact)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{coEq}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\sigma^{-1}\phi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

and this provides again a coequifying diagram in the following with the coequifying 1-cell being cartesian.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(K_{2},a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\textbf{coEq}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),\langle\sigma^{-1}\phi\rangle)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{1},\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})},\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{2},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}

From this together with
Lemma

2.2.6
, we see even more directly than in the bi-accessible case why the pseudoslice over the bicompacts is bifiltered and bicofinal thanks to internalization of finite bicolimits; this condenses to the following result:

Lemma 3.3.5
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is a finitely bipresentable 2-category, then for any
B
the
\sigma
-pair
(\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B,{\bf Cart}_{B})
is
\sigma
-filtered; as a consequence, the pseudococone
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B
is bifiltered and cofinal in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\Downarrow B
relatively to
{\bf Cart}_{B}
, and we have

B\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow B

Now we are going to prove that, as like as in the 1-dimensional case, existence of bicolimits in a finitely accessible category is actually equivalent to existence of bilimits. This makes use of the binerve functor.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

For a finitely bipresentable 2-category
\mathcal{B}
we have a biadjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

where
L
denotes the left biKan extension
L=\textup{biLan}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}\iota
. In particular, we can exhibit
\mathcal{B}
as a bireflective sub-2-category of
{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\mathcal{B}
is bicocomplete, so we can compute the pointwise left biKan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{\omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

and this pointwise extension expresses as the weighted bicolimit as discussed in
Proposition

1.3.2
and
1.3.3
,

{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}\iota(F)={\textup{bicolim}}^{N_{\text{\char 136\relax}}(F)}\iota

Though such a result is rather standard, let us check that this defines a left biadjoint to
\nu
, for the sake of completeness. For
B
in
\mathcal{B}
and
F
in
{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[{\textup{biLan}}_{\text{\char 136\relax}}\iota(F),B]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[{\textup{bicolim}}^{N_{\text{\char 136\relax}}(F)}\iota,B]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[N_{\text{\char 136\relax}}(F),\mathcal{B}[\iota,B]\big]

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]\big[F,\nu_{B}\big]

∎

Corollary 3.3.7
.

Any finitely bipresentable 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is bicomplete, and moreover, finite weighted bilimits commute with bifiltered bicolimits.

Proof.

The first item comes from the general fact that bireflective sub-2-categories are closed under bilimits as seen at
Proposition

1.4.1
. In particular, if we see a finitely birepresentable 2-category
\mathcal{B}
as a category of pseudofunctor from its binerve, bilimits are computed pointwisely as in the pseudofunctor category.

Moreover we saw at
Lemma

3.2.13
that the binerve preserves bifiltered bicolimits. Hence both bilimits and bifiltered bicolimits of
\mathcal{B}
are computed in
{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]
, where both are pointwise. But finite weighted bilimits commute with bifiltered bicolimit in
{\bf Cat}
, hence so do they in the pseudofunctor category, and in
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Theorem 3.3.8
.

The following are equivalent:

-

\mathcal{B}
is finitely bipresentable,

-

\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible and bicomplete.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
was already proven, thus we concentrate on the other one. Consider the nerve pseudofunctor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

we know that this is fully faithful, because
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, and it preserve all weighted bilimits, as all nerves do. Thus, we can apply
Theorem

5.2.3
, which we will prove later in the paper, and infer that the nerve has a left adjoint. As a result,
\mathcal{B}
is reflective and thus has all bicolimits.
∎

Remark 3.3.9
.

Beware however that arbitrary small bicolimits are not preserved by the binerve pseudofunctor. In particular, even when seeing objects of
B
as pseudofunctors
(\mathcal{B}_{\omega})^{{\rm op}}\rightarrow{\bf Cat}
, arbitrary bicolimits cannot be calculated pointwisely as bicolimits in the pseudofunctor 2-category - though bifiltered ones are so.

To conclude this section, we give a process to construct finitely bipresentable 2-categories from other ones. It is well known that, for a locally finitely presentable category, a reflective subcategory whose embedding is accessible is locally finitely presentable itself. We give here the corresponding statement; but before that, the following lemma, with weaker condition, gives some insight to bicompact objects in this context:

Lemma 3.3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finitely bipresentable 2-category, and
R:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
preserving bifiltered bicolimits with a left bi-adjoint
L
. Then
L
sends bicompacts on bicompacts.

Proof.

For
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
with
(I,\Sigma)
bifiltered, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[L(K),\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i)]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[K,R(\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,F(i))]

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{B}[K,\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,RF(i)]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{B}[K,RF(i)]

\displaystyle\simeq\underset{i\in I}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\,\mathcal{A}[L(K),F(i)]

Hence each
L(K)
is bicompact in
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

3.4
A criterion to recognize finitely bipresentable
2
-categories

Definition 3.4.1
.

A a small sub
2
-category
\iota:\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is a
strong generator
if its associated nerve

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[\mathcal{G}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

is biconservative, that is, reflects equivalences.

Remark 3.4.2
.

Dense generators are strong, of course. Under very weak and natural assumptions on the ambient
2
-category
\mathcal{B}
, eso-generators in the sense of
[
Str82b
,
Str82a
]
are also strong. In practise, we do not know a reasonable
2
-category where eso-generators are not strong.

Theorem 3.4.3

(Recognition theorem for finitely bipresentable
2
-categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be locally small
2
-category with weigthed bicolimits. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{B}
is finitely bipresentable,

2.

\mathcal{B}
has a strong generator
\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
made of bicompact objects.

Proof.

Because dense generators are in particular strong, the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
is trivial. We focus on the other implication. Our proof is inspired by
[
Kel82
, 7.2 (i)]
and
[
AR94
, 1.11]
. Call
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
the closure of
\mathcal{G}
under finite weighted bicolimits and consider the obvious inclusions,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\mathcal{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Of course
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
consists of compact objects, because of
Proposition

3.1.12
and duly,
j
preserves finite weighted bicolimits by construction. Also,
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
is a strong generator too. In order to finish the proof it is enough to show that every object in
\mathcal{B}
is a
\sigma
-filtered bicolimit of objects in
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
, indeed by the
Lemma

2.2.6
this entails that every objects is a bifiltered bicolimit of objects in
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
B
be an object in
\mathcal{B}
and consider the canonical diagram

(\bar{\mathcal{G}}\Downarrow B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{B}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{B}.

So it suffices to show that
B
is indeed equivalent to the
\sigma
-filtered
\sigma
-bicolimit
\sigma_{\bf Cart}{\textup{bicolim}}\pi_{B}
for the class of cartesian morphisms. In order to see this, consider the diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf ps}[\bar{\mathcal{G}}^{{\rm op}},{\bf Cat}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{j}}

Because
j
preserves finite weighted bicolimits,
\nu_{j}(B)=\mathcal{B}[j,B]
is lex. By applying
[
DDS18
, 4.2.7]
, we know that
\nu_{j}(\sigma_{\bf Cart}{\textup{bicolim}}\pi_{B})\simeq\nu_{j}(B)
. Now, using the conservativity of
\nu_{j}
and the fact that it must preserve
\sigma
-filtered bicolimits, this shows that
B
must be the bicolimit of
\pi_{B}
.

∎

Corollary 3.4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finitely bipresentable
2
-category. Let
\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
be a strong generator of compact objects. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
coincides with the closure of
\mathcal{G}
under finite weighted bicolimits.

Proof.

We only need to show that every object in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
is a finite weighted bicolimit of objects in
\mathcal{G}
. Let
B
be an object in
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
. Using the proof of the previous theorem, we know that
B\simeq\sigma_{\bf Cart}{\textup{bicolim}}\;\pi_{B}
. In particular, we have a
1
-cell (which is an equivalence)

B\to\sigma_{\bf Cart}{\textup{bicolim}}\;\pi_{B}.

Since the diagram is
\sigma
-filtered and
B
is bicompact, we obtain on the spot that
B
must be a retract of one of the
G
in
\mathcal{G}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finitely bipresentable
2
-category. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
, the full
2
-subcategory of
\lambda
-compact objects is the closure of
\mathcal{B}_{\omega}
under
\lambda
-small weighted bicolimits.

Proof.

Using the
\lambda
-version of
Theorem

3.4.3
is is evident that an finitely bipresentable is
\lambda
bipresentable for every higher
\lambda
. Then we use the
\lambda
-version of
Corollary

3.4.4
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finitely bipresentable
2
-category. Then every object is
\lambda
-compact for some
\lambda
.

Proof.

The canonical diagram must be
\lambda
-small for some
\lambda
. Then we use that
\lambda
-small bicolimits of bicompact objects are still bicompact.
∎

Remark 3.4.7
.

The previous Corollary is true at a higher level of generality. Indeed if
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, every object is still the bicolimit of its canonical diagram, and
Proposition

3.1.12
still applies.

3.5
Bi-accessibility of pseudo-arrow category

Before relating our theory of accessibility to the theory of flatness, as will be done in the next section, we would like to describe a basic operations on finitely bipresentables categories, namely the notion of
category of arrows
. First, recall that the
pseudoarrow 2-category
of a 2-category
\mathcal{B}
is the 2-category of pseudofunctors
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
, which is moreover equipped with a canonical 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

where
\lambda
is a pseudonatural transformation whose component at

-

an arrow
f
is the 1-cell
\lambda_{f}=f:{\rm dom}(f)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(f)

-

a pseudosquare
(u,u^{\prime},\alpha):f_{1}\rightarrow f_{2}
is the invertible 2-cell
\lambda_{(u,u^{\prime},\alpha)}=\alpha

-

a morphism of pseudosquares
(\phi,\phi^{\prime}):(u_{1},u^{\prime}_{1},\alpha_{1})\Rightarrow(u_{2},u^{\prime}_{2},\alpha_{2})
is the equality of 2-cells
\alpha_{2}f_{2}*\phi=\phi^{\prime}*f_{1}\alpha_{1}
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

If
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bi-accessible, then so is
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
. If moreover
\mathcal{B}
is finitely bipresentable, then so is
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
, with a biequivalence

({\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}])_{\omega}\simeq{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}_{\omega}]

Proof.

From what was said before,
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
inherits bilimits and bicolimits existing in
\mathcal{B}
, hence in particular its filtered bicolimits. In particular, as soon as
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
is proven to be finitely bi-accessible, it will be automatically finitely bipresentable. The properties of the domain and codomain functors process from the fact they preserve both bilimits and bicolimits. We then have to prove bi-accessibility.

First, we prove that bicompact arrows have bicompact domains and codomains. For the codomain, let
k:{\rm dom}(k)\rightarrow{\rm cod}(k)
be a bicompact arrow in
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
and a bifiltered diagram
F:I\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
. Then for any
a:{\rm cod}(k)\rightarrow{\textup{bicolim}}F
defines an identity 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,1_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where
1_{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}={\textup{bicolim}}\,1_{F}
. Hence for
k
is bicompact we have a factorization of
(ak,a):k\rightarrow 1_{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}
through some
q_{i}:F(i)\rightarrow{\textup{bicolim}}\,F

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,1_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ak}

where
b^{\prime}
provides a desired lift. Hence
{\rm cod}(k)
is bicompact.

For the domain, consider the bifiltered colimit
{\rm dom}(k)\simeq{\textup{bicolim}}\;\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow{\rm dom}(k)
, which exhibits
k
as the induced arrow
k=\langle ka\rangle_{a\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow k}
. Hence we have a bifiltered colimit in
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]

k\simeq\underset{a\in\mathcal{B}_{\omega}\downarrow k}{{\textup{bicolim}}}\;ka

so that we have a factorization for some
a:K\rightarrow{\rm dom}(k)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ka}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

exhibiting
{\rm dom}(k)
as a pseudoretract of a bicompact, and hence as a bicompact object itself.

Now we prove that any arrow between bicompact object is bicompact in
{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
. Take
k:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
with
K,\,K^{\prime}
bicompact, and
F:I\rightarrow{\bf ps}[2,\mathcal{B}]
a bifiltered diagram. Then for any square as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm dom}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm cod}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\atop\simeq}

bicompactness of
K
and
K^{\prime}
provides us respectively with a upper and lower lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm dom}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm cod}\,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}\atop\simeq}

and we want to construct further lifts with a vertical arrow between them. But precomposing respectively the upper lift with
F(i)
and the lower lift with
k
provides two distincts lifts of a same arrow
{\textup{bicolim}}\,Fa
as seen below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm cod}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,Fa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,F*\beta\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}*k\atop\simeq}

Now for
K
is bicompact, those two lifts admit a common refinement for some
d:i\rightarrow j
,
d^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow j
equipped with an invertible 2-cell
\gamma
, and in the diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm dom}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm cod}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,F(d^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,F(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,q_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{bicolim}}\,F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{j}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(d)\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,\theta_{d}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{d}\atop\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,\theta_{d}\atop\simeq}

we can extract the following lift

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,F(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm dom}\,F(d)b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm cod}\,F(d^{\prime})b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma b*F(d){\rm cod}\,F(d)*\beta\atop\simeq}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm dom}\,F(j)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(j^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textup{bicolim}}\,{\rm cod}\,F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm cod}\,F(i_{2}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
